pneumonia.2 He had returned to the country of his birth following the overthrow of the Tsar (Emperor of Russia) in February 1917, only to experience
the seizure of State power by Lenin's Communist Party that November
(the so-called October Revolution) and the imposition of dictatorial policies antithetical to mutual aid.3 Having been harassed out of Moscow in
the summer of 1918 by Communist government bureaucrats, Kropotkin's
"involuntary exile" to a small village outside the capital was plagued by
privation, but his creativity never wavered.4 "During the two days I spent
in the Kropotkin household," Goldman relates,

I learned more of his personal life than during all the years that I

had known him. Even his closest friends were not aware that Peter

Kropotkin was an artist . . . of much talent. Among his efforts I discovered a collection of drawings of great merit. . . . And now he lay

on his couch, in the little workroom, as if peacefully asleep, his face

as kindly in death as it had been in life.5

Read praised Mutual Aid as "possibly [Kropotkin's] greatest book"
and reprints passages from it throughout his compendium.6 Most importantly for our purposes, he ends his Selections with a subsection, "Art and
Society," excerpted from The Conquest of Bread (1892).7 Here we encounter
further evidence of Kropotkin's conviction that the arts are important.
He laments the divorce of art from the communal life of the people under
capitalism, writing "only when cities, territories, nations, or groups of
nations, will have renewed their harmonious life, will art be able to draw
its inspiration from ideals held in common."8 "Art," he declared, "must be
bound up with industry by a thousand intermediate degrees, blended, so to
say, as . . . the great Socialist poet [William] Morris (1834–1896) . . . proved
so often and so well. Everything that surrounds man, in the street, in the
interior and exterior of public monuments, must be of pure artistic form.
But this can only happen in a society in which all enjoy comfort and leisure.
Then only shall we see art associations, of which each member will find
room for his capacity."9 This visionary indebtedness to Morris, founder of
the international Arts and Crafts movement, merits further exploration.10
Kropotkin's first contact with Morris dates to 1886.11 After a daring
escape from Russian imprisonment in 1876, Kropotkin spent a harried
period in exile, mostly in Switzerland and France. Anarchist-related activities led to expulsion from Switzerland in 1881, followed by a brief sojourn
in England before relocation to France. In 1883 the French government sentenced him to five years' imprisonment for his politics and, upon gaining
early release in January 1886, it was clear he could no longer remain in
that country, so he accepted the invitation of a group of London-based
anarchists to help found a newspaper to promote anarchist communism
(Kropotkin would cofound Freedom in 1886).12 Anarchism was then gaining
ground in the United Kingdom, and the involvement of a renowned figure
already famous for his scientific work as well as a myriad of anarchistrelated books, pamphlets, articles, and journalistic contributions was cause
for celebration. Within a few days of his arrival in early March, Kropotkin
was invited to speak at an event organized by the Socialist League (1884–
1901) honouring the Paris Commune (1871), and this is where he met Morris,
who was the League's driving force and editor of its journal, Commonweal.13
Morris was an antiparliamentarian "communist" who, Ruth Kinna
summarizes, shared Kropotkin's political vision of decentralized federalism but declined to join the movement on the grounds that it was tainted
by "individualist" tendencies (advocating "terrorism" and "violent revolution" was also, tactically, problematic).14 Nevertheless, affinities often
trump differences. After their first meeting, Morris wrote to a friend

relating "Kropotkin's new come [sic] from prison and I made his acquaintance . . . I like him very much."15 Thereafter Kropotkin was a frequent
visitor to Morris's home, Kelmscott House, where he mixed with artists,
printers, embroiderers, book illustrators, writers, poets, playwrights, and
radicals.16 He also befriended Morris's daughter, May (1862–1938), who was
then in charge of the embroidery division of his artisan-based multifaceted
business, Morris and Co. (1875–1896).17 Kropotkin was not the only anarchist Morris maintained cordial relations with. He hosted anarchist communism's other leading theorist, Elisée Reclus (1830–1905), at Kelmscott
House18 and collaborated with Victor Dave (1847–1922), a Belgian who had
fled persecution to London, to co-author a Socialist League pamphlet, A
Short Account of the Paris Commune (1886).19 Jean Grave (1854–1939), editor
of the anarchist communist journals La Révolte (1887–1894) and its successor, Les Temps Nouveaux (1895–1914), was on familiar enough terms to petition Morris for illustrations in 1890 and solicit a frontispiece design for a
Les Temps Nouveaux portfolio of artists' lithographs in 1896, not long before
Morris died.20 And, in 1891, Morris assisted Louise Michel (1830–1905), who
had fought in the Paris Commune, to launch an anarchist-inspired school
for children in London.21 In a friendly letter to the anarchist communist
typesetter Wolf Wess (1861–1946) agreeing to support Kropotkin's newspaper, Freedom, Morris gently suggested most anarchists were "socialists,
only somewhat wrong-headed, if I may say so, as to tactics."22 This is the
spirit in which he forged affinities with the movement and, in 1896, backed
an unsuccessful proposal that anarchist organizations participate in the
Second International.23

Morris's commitment to artisan-based practices encompassing
weaving tapestries and rugs, decorative tiles, furniture manufacture, interior decoration, jewelry-making, wallpaper and stained-glass production,
woodblock engraving, textiles, illustration, and book publishing, was driven
by his conviction that artisanship was the means through which workers
in the decentralized communist future would foster a society in which
work was pleasurable and artistry permeated everyday life.24 Similarly, as
Robyn Roslak observes, anarchist communists also advocated "a future
society structured around small, self-governing communes whose economies would be based on an alliance of intensive agriculture, light industry,
and skilled craftsmanship."25 In fact, Reclus and Kropotkin "took an enormous interest in artisanal culture" and likened craftspeople engaged in
"intelligent and careful labour" to "artists," in contrast to factory workers
reduced to "auxiliaries to the machine."26 Artisanship, they believed, could
liberate work, and this accords with the passages selected by Read from

The Conquest of Bread. The foundational bond uniting Morris with the
anarchist communists was their shared admiration for the medieval era's
guild-based economies and cooperative artisanship as showcased in Mutual
Aid.27 Whatever its faults, this offered a model for the communist future
worth emulating.28

We should not be surprised, then, to learn that anarchists took notice
of Morris's last great endeavor, the Kelmscott Press (1890–1896). Morris's
undertaking was inspired, in part, by his substantial collection of medieval
illuminated manuscripts and fifteenth-century printed books.29 "I began
producing books," Morris modestly related, "with the hope of producing some which would have a definite claim to beauty while at the same
time they should be easy to read."30 To this end ornamentation, specialty
typefaces and "beautiful and imaginative pictures" were employed to
create "the most harmonious decoration possible to the printed book."31
For his part, Morris designed Kelmscott Press typefaces and ornamental borders. Artists contributed illustrations and a host of craftspeople
engaged in carving woodblocks, setting type, cutting paper, binding books,
and other tasks. (Kathy Ferguson provides a succinct overview of the production process in her groundbreaking study of anarchist letterpresses).32
The results exemplified the Arts and Crafts values expounded in Morris's
1892 novel, News from Nowhere: An Epoch of Rest (the frontispiece of the
Kelmscott Press edition depicts Kelmscott House).33 News from Nowhere
narrates the impressions of a Socialist League radical who wakes up in a
free society where property is abolished and artisanal manufacturing reigns
supreme (an abridged version of the story first appeared in installments
in the Commonweal between January 11 and October 4, 1890).34 Central
government has been abolished and the Houses of Parliament are storage
facilities for manure.35 Industries that degraded the environment and
rendered work oppressive are no more.36 Nature flourishes, poverty has
been eliminated, and large cities have contracted or disappeared entirely in
favour of small villages.37 Work is voluntary and pleasure-focused, leisure
is celebrated, free love reigns supreme, men and women live as equals,
and everyone can, potentially, become an "artist" thanks to artisanship.38
Referencing News from Nowhere, the UK's leading anarchist communist
journal, Freedom (June 1891), deemed Morris "in character a born-anarchist"
and his story certainly dovetails with anarchist values, not only in terms
of content, but also in terms of style.39 As Marie-Louise Berneri points out
in her critical study of utopias, News from Nowhere refuses any prescriptive agenda: instead, its attractiveness seduces us into desiring the future
Morris imagines.40

This brings me to the politics of the book and Bonzo's Arts and Crafts–
inflected illustrations for Mutual Aid. Referencing beauty in nature and
art, Morris associated the urge to rebel with the desire to expand beauty's
compass through an artisanal revolution against industrial capitalism and
ecological degradation.41 Stimulating the imagination, beauty was an agitational, desire-producing social force, as opposed to 'naturalism' (realism),
which blunted the impetus to radical social change (Morris was indebted
to Kropotkin, who mounted a similar critique).42 Like his mentor, Morris,
Kelmscott Press illustrator and fellow communist Walter Crane (1845–1915)
also regarded beauty from a political point of view. Engaging with "many
pressing questions outside the immediate province of design," Crane counselled, the radical illustrator would

discover that art leads him to its source in the mind and the life of

humanity, and that when it is a living thing it is always the fullest

expression of that mind and that life, and its colours are the colours

of the good and the evil of it. He may find himself between the wings

of those spirits of light and darkness, which, under whatever forms

and names, like night and day, constantly overshadow the world and

dispute the territory of the human mind between them—the one

pointing to despair and indolence, the other to hope and strife.43

Illustration and ornamentation should be pleasing to the eye, the object of
design being the realization of "ideal beauty [rather] than literal fact."44 In
sum, the aesthetic attractiveness of a book could simultaneously amplify
its critical content by virtue of its beauty, and the seductive power of that
beauty. It could, in effect, prefigure a world transformed, even as it pointed
to "evils of all kinds" and the need for transformation. Expounding on a
communist future in his statement, "Socialism and Artists," published in
Grave's La Révolte (1893), Crane postulates:

What a beautiful social order will rise . . . when the artistic sense, the

love of beauty, the spirit of invention, the development of all our faculties, forever freed from the torments of our present livelihood, of forced

work and evils of all kinds which are inseparable from our current

existence, can develop freely, ennobling and embellishing life. . . . All

the arts will come together to celebrate through new and powerful

creations the happiness of mankind, permanently emancipated.45

News from Nowhere includes a pivotal chapter, "How the Change
Came," concerning the working-class revolution that ends capitalism. In
the course of being serialised in the Commonweal on May 24, 1890, it was

Walter Crane, Solidarity of Labour, 1890

accompanied by one of Crane's most popular illustrations, "Solidarity of
Labour: Labour's May Day"46 []. "Solidarity of Labour" depicts the
angelic personification of "freedom" unfurling "equality" and "fraternity"
while workers personifying the continents join hands in a celebratory dance
around the globe. The earth is not desiccated; it is fecund. Sheaves of wheat

rise from the soil beneath the feet of dancing workers as "freedom" breaks
from the picture frame, signifying she embodies a transcendent, unifying
idea worth struggling for. That struggle, as narrated by Morris, entails not
only overcoming State power, but also industrial capitalism and its attendant, ecocide. Cooperative social order is synonymous with the spontaneity
of the natural order, and Crane's joyous revelers 'perform' this relationship
as they down tools to join hands and cavort 'round and 'round the globe.
"How the Change Came" describes a protracted period of social strife,
protests, government repression, armed insurrection, and mass strikes.
"Solidarity of Labour: Labour's May Day" depicts the "instinct for freedom"
in continuum with the "happiness of a free life" that eluded the oppressed
as they embarked on their struggle, but which grew, organically, in the
course of "the Change."47 Appropriately, on May 12, 1890, Commonweal
revolutionaries also celebrated May with an evening of dancing: reality
prefigures art, and vice versa.48

A few years later, in 1892, Crane contributed another striking illustration for the prospectus of Louise Michel's short-lived International School
[] which prematurely closed after being compromised by undercover police operatives.49 His collaboration with the Paris Commune's
most famous anarchist was an outgrowth, in part, of his friendship with
Kropotkin, whose art-related views in The Conquest of Bread guided Crane
as he wrote "Socialism and Artists."50 Michel founded her school to
educate the children of political exiles in the spirit of anarchist fraternitéé.
"By taking on all these little French, English, German children, and teaching
them languages," she related, "I wanted to enable them to know, and later
understand one another, so that eventually, through the communion of
ideas, nations might eventually hate one another less and learn to love one
another."51 To ensure accessibility, the school was sustained by donations,
apart from a small fee for language instruction (students living in residence
were also charged room and board).52 Constance Bantman underlines just
how radical Michel's project was:

The teaching was based on [Mikhail] Bakunin's educational principles, with an emphasis on scientific and rational methods, personal

dignity, and individual independence, as well as clear references to

anarchism. One visitor, for instance, recalled a history lesson illustrated by images of the hanged Chicago martyrs and the Communards

being shot. Corporal punishment was not used to sanction disruptive pupils, who were expelled or moved to a lower class instead.

This clearly defies the stereotypical assumption of anarchist lack of

Walter Crane, Cover for Louis Michel's International School Prospectus,

discipline. Above all, the school aimed to "develop among children the

principles of humanity and justice essential in the twentieth century."

French, English, German, Music, Drawing, Sewing, and Etching were

among the subjects taught. The influence of the French libertarian

educationist Paul Robin can be seen in the notion of "integral education," both physical and intellectual. Technical teaching was included,

as well as adult classes, especially English lessons for foreigners

arriving in London. Michel taught the piano. [The school] signified

the creation of a free radical and prefigurative space, which in turn

has been identified as a practice that has proved especially congenial

for feminists within the anarchist movement.53

Crane's depiction is surrounded by slogans—"From each according to his capacity"; "To each according to his needs"; "Liberty. Equality.
Fraternity"; "La Solidaritéé Humaine" (Human Solidarity)—proclaiming
the school's anarchist communist values. Liberty (donning a popular revolutionary symbol of emancipation, the liberty bonnet)54 is the agent of
education, who holds a glowing lamp of "truth" aloft as she plucks the
fruits of knowledge from an overflowing "tree of life" and distributes them
to eager boys and girls, three of whom climb the ladder of "enlightenment"
to pick fruit themselves. Children participate in their own learning facilitated by adults, and the branches covered in foliage and laden with nature's
bounty signify Michel's school is a social space where learning prospers,
as does freedom. True knowledge is beautiful because it integrates teachers, pupils and society into a larger harmony, a natural order which is
healthy and thriving (cf. Mutual Aid). These values are heightened by the
rhythmic interplay of arabesque lines and compositional arrangement, the
grace of Crane's figures, their elegance, and their vitality. Idealizing beauty
unfurls the announcement, "International School Conducted by Louise
Michel—Prospectus," as it agitates for the school's realization and much
more besides.

Additional insights can be gleaned from the Kelmscott Press edition of
Morris's cautionary tale, The Story of the Glittering Plain (1894), which was
illustrated by Crane. Written during or not long after News from Nowhere,
the story relates a hero's induction into a utopian "glittering plain" where
people are immortal, only to discover perfection has arrested life: there is
nothing to strive for or struggle against. The hero then battles to escape the
glittering plain, choosing life and its imperfections over utopian absolutism.55 His choice accords with the world portrayed in News from Nowhere,
where the future remains a work in progress and the visitor from the past
returns to fight "with whatsoever pain and labour needs must be, to build
up little by little the new day of fellowship, and rest, and happiness."56
Crane's twenty-three illustrations, executed as woodcuts, are bordered
by ornate interweaving floral motifs designed by Morris. [] Visual
cornucopias envelop us in a political fable that unfolds through the act of
reading, in keeping with Crane's conviction that "beautiful and symbolic
form" could communicate "social ideals."57

William Morris and Walter Crane (illustrator), The Story of the Glittering
Plain or the Land of Living Men, 1894

Two years after Morris's death, in 1898, Kelmscott Press equipment
(minus woodblocks and typeface) was purchased by a second Arts and
Crafts undertaking, the Guild of Handicraft (1888–1907), and transferred
to a new abode in London's bedraggled East End to help launch the Essex
House Press.58 Spearheaded by Morris's close friend and fellow radical,
architect C.R. Ashbee (1863–1942), the Guild employed artisans skilled in
carpentry, metal work, printing, interior decoration, and other trades, and
in 1898 it was flourishing.59 The anarchist communist Edward Carpenter
(1844–1929), who was part of his circle (and a good friend of Morris),
played a central role in Ashbee's project from its inception.60 In accord
with Carpenter's values, the Guild was organized as an artisan cooperative
(forty voting members) with educational programs and an emphasis on
communal "comradeship."61 Relocation in 1902 to the countryside village
of Chipping Campden in the Cotswolds, where its craftspeople and their
families could reacquaint themselves with "Mother Earth" (Ashbee), was
the final step.62 News from Nowhere envisages a society where people heal
the earth by renouncing industrialism and large-scale urbanism to live
more simply. Symbolically, the story begins with London's Thames transformed from a polluted watercourse lined with "smoke-vomiting chimneys"
into a bucolic river bordered by beautiful villages, teaming with salmon.63
Just as symbolically, it ends when a "black cloud" returns its protagonist

to his "dingy" home in a "shabby London suburb."64 Echoing Morris,
Carpenter promoted the "simple life," not only in writing, but by living
self-sufficiently, tilling a market garden and residing in a modest cottage
he designed and built in the countryside outside of Sheffield.65 When the
Guild of Handicraft left London for an "unspoilt mediaeval wool town"
free of industrial pollution, slums and overcrowding, it was emulating
Carpenter and realizing Morris's vision on a far more substantive scale.66
Economic challenges determined the Guild would dissolve in 1907, but
many former members stayed on and ran their own workshops.67

Over its short lifetime (1898–1910), Essex House Press produced
ninety-four titles with specialty typeface designed by Ashbee, some
illustrated (two by Crane). Several discussed the Guild of Handicraft, its
mission, and social purpose, a congruence in which each publication was
testimony, as Ferguson puts it, "to the politics that held them together."68
This expanded to anti-colonial activism when, in 1907, Indian anarchist
Ananda Coomaraswamy (1877–1947) and his wife, master weaver Edith
Partridge (1872–1952), purchased one of Morris's presses from the Guild of
Handicraft.69 For three more years they continued producing books and
pamphlets with Ashbee's assistance in their home near Chipping Campden
under the imprint Kelmscott & Essex House. Their first publication was
The Deeper Meaning of the Struggle (1907), in which Coomaraswamy argued
India's independence could only be realized by rejecting the industrial
model of modernity and renewing the subcontinent's pre-colonial artisan-based economy.70 In subsequent books and pamphlets, he would broaden
the scope of this "post-industrial" revolution by calling for a parallel transformation in the West, a prospect which captivated Ashbee and many in
the anarchist movement.71

Inspired by the example of Morris (and Ashbee's Guild of Handicraft),
in 1894 an anarchist communist couple, Lucien (1886–1944) and Esther
Pissarro (1870–1951), formed a tight-knit 'cooperative' of two and launched
London's Eragny Press (1894–1914).72 The couple worked as a team, from
book design to wood engraving, typesetting, and printing, with Lucien creating the illustrations.73 Their ambition was economic self-sufficiency, but
the endeavour was an artistic outlet, first and foremost. "Either you work as
an artist," Lucien reasoned, "or look upon yourselves as a tradesman . . . if
you try to be one, you will lose your quality as an artist. Thus, it is better . . .
to follow your artistic feelings without thinking of sales . . . we don't work
in order to earn a living, but because we cannot help it. If by chance you
earn some money, all the better, but that is not the purpose."74 Lucien was
an accomplished painter and illustrator whose father, impressionist painter

(and fellow anarchist communist) Camille Pissarro, encouraged the couple
in this attitude.75 The Eragny Press was "reclaiming the book for beauty,
and the making of it as a work of art."76 Though none of its thirty-two
publications were overtly political, they do reflect an anarchist communist
sensibility attuned to nature, the social value of beauty, and the creative
agency of the artist.

While producing books, Lucien painted neo-impressionist canvases
(predominantly agrarian landscapes) and frequently exhibited with this
anarchist-inspired offshoot of the impressionist movement, which first
coalesced in 1886 (Lucien exhibited in the group's debut exhibition).77 The
exacting style, involving the application of uniform dots of contrasting
pigments of colour according to a scheme of visual harmony in concert
with curvilinear lines and rhythmic forms, gained numerous adherents in
France, Belgium, and elsewhere.78 Additionally, Lucien contributed illustrations to a number of anarchist publications, notably the militant, class
war–oriented journals Le Père peinard (1889–1902) and The Torch: A Journal
of Anarchist Communism (1891–1895). Subjects varied, from scenes of class
conflict, poverty, and injustice to countryside depictions of peasants going
about their affairs without an oppressor in sight. He also followed debates
in Les Temps Nouveaux, where he shared the Eragny Press's position on
art. Cautioning against hierarchies based on an artwork's "direct utility to
propaganda," Lucien argued "pure beauty" was revolutionary because it
broadens people's aesthetic conceptions. Distinctions drawn between "art
for art's sake" (bad) and "social art" (good) were false, as the supposed
dichotomy denied the socialness of aesthetic values, regardless of content.
Restricting anarchists to producing didactic art dictated by the sensibilities
of "the masses" was counterproductive because it shut down the possibility of invention. By contrast, "difficult" art born of the artist's imagination
might gain meaning for others gradually, through education, but that, after
all, was the nature of "progress."79 For Lucien and Esther Pissarro, "reclaiming the book for beauty" involved the free play of innovation in concordance
with the artist's sensibility: this imparted the work with originality and
served as an index of autonomy from the demands of capitalism ("follow
your artistic feelings without thinking of sales").80 Lucien's defence dovetails with the position of many anarchist artists concerning the socially
liberating potential of aesthetics contra capitalism and its authoritarian
cultural constructs, a "politics of form" that Patricia Leighten explores in
The Liberation of Painting: Modernism and Anarchism in Avant-Guerre Paris.81
The couple's first book, The Queen of Fishes (1895), exemplifies their
anarchist communist ethos on multiple levels.82 The Queen of Fishes is a

fairy tale from northern France which relates the story of a girl and boy
who are sent to the woods to catch fish (the girl) or cut wood (the boy)
every day. Their growing friendship mirrors their increasing identity with
a river filled with fishes and the forest through which it flows. Eventually,
the boy recognizes the girl is the Queen of Fishes (whom he has dreamt
about) and she reveals to him that he is the King of the Forest (as he
was in his dream). The two describe the harmony sustaining their respective 'realms'—river and woods—in terms that resonate with Mutual Aid's
themes. The boy's uncle, a cruel woodcutter who exploits the riches of the
forest, comes across the teenagers conversing and, angry at his nephew for
refusing to cut wood from living trees, brutalizes him. He also realizes the
girl (who likewise has refused to capture fish, despite knowing she will be
beaten by her parents) is the Queen of Fishes and promises to set a net to
entrap her the next time she metaphorizes. A few days later, the worried
boy finds the Queen of Fishes caught in his uncle's net and sets her free,
which enrages his uncle. At this point he declares himself King of the Forest
and transforms into a gigantic oak. The woodcutter vows to kill the King of
the Forest and rounds up a group of men to cut and burn down the woods.
The Queen of Fishes then pleads with the river fairies to intervene: "She
told them how the beautiful forest was being destroyed so that the land
would be dry and barren and the river springs dried up and upon hearing
this they were persuaded to help her."83 The fairies reveal their displeasure
to the men, who refuse to carry out the uncle's orders. Once the forest has
been saved, the Queen becomes a nymph and the King becomes a fawn, so
that they may marry and live the rest of their lives in peace with the forest,
the river, and its protectors.

Lucien enthused to his father that The Queen of Fishes was an "original" conception born of his unique aesthetic sensibility, as evidenced in
the panel depicting the girl returning fish to the river.84 Drawing and then
carving his woodblock depictions in "a style completely natural,"85 he and
Esther adapted harmonizing color values to the printing process, resulting
in a new variation of the Arts and Crafts illustrated book following neoimpressionist tenets. For example, in one instance Lucien incorporates a
decorative "picture frame" composed of complementary pigments (red and
green) to amplify his illustration's predominating colours [], just as
numerous neoimpressionists did with their canvases.86 Marcella D. Genz
adds: "Besides employing impressionist colours and gothic gold, the illustrations are rustically simple and natural without affectation. The figures
are not stylized but are drawn and cut on the wood based on Lucien's vision
of the characters, not in imitation of someone else's style."87 Departing from

Esther Pissarro and Lucien Pissarro (illustrator), The Queen of Fishes,

Morris's mediaeval-based designs, Lucien bordered his colour illustrations
with organic 'art nouveau' ornamentation, in accord with the rhythmic,
harmony-inducing arabesques suffusing neo-impressionist paintings.88
"It is not an elaborate entwining border, Genz observes, "but is composed
of a gentle whiplash." The Queen of Fishes, she concludes, "is an integrated
whole; all of the parts, both text and illustration, belong to each other."89
Lucien's illustrations enhance the story's message of interspecies mutual
aid (the teenagers' miraculous transformations enact these relationships),
and ecological responsibility on a visual level. The world he illustrates
is beautiful because its protagonists are integral to it. I am reminded of
Kropotkin's injunction in Mutual Aid that humanity recognize itself as
but one species within a vast planetary biosphere and further, through
mutual aid, the well-being of the whole: this is an aesthetic act, as much
as anything else.90

The illustrated Mutual Aid similarly mobilizes beauty to agitate for
an anarchist society whose immanence is embodied in the complex web of
relationships out of which life itself is constituted. It comes as no surprise,

then, to learn that N.O. Bonzo looks back to anarchism's Arts and Crafts
era for inspiration. Here, then, are the artist's closing reflections.

AA: When did you first encounter the work of Crane and Morris and what
drew you to it?
N.O. Bonzo: Crane and Morris's work have had such enduring power that I
encountered a lot of their work even before attributing it to them and learning about the broader movements they were associated with. I was drawn
to them for the same reasons many anarchists are. They represent a very
important push back against the cheapening of art—its degeneration into
a purely commercial or upper-class elite enterprise drained of any political potential. Their literary and artistic productions beautifully articulate
a desire for liberation: they give us vivid imagery of the world we wish to
create, complete with signposts on how to get there.

AA: I discuss beauty's potential to awaken our critical consciousness.
Can you elaborate on this issue as it relates to your art?
Bonzo: Before I focused solely on illustration, I was a graffiti artist: street
art gets you thinking a lot about the impact that any decorative feature or
artwork has on the person observing it. You can get different responses
by depicting a towering grey building in the Brutalist tradition91 under
a bridge or by spray-painting a building with a colorful FTP (fuck the
police). Many in the Arts and Crafts movement talked about how capitalism contributes nothing to enriching our lived experience. The aesthetic
of the capitalist environments we live in often feels completely out of our
control: what can you do about all this utilitarian, profit-driven concrete
with exhaust stains on it? One of my important goals, beyond my own
impetus to be creative, is to bring imagery into the world that enriches
people's everyday experiences by depicting alternatives to the capitalist
State status-quo. My illustrations in Mutual Aid are meant to be rich and
organic, featuring daily life without alienation.

AA: Would it be fair to say you are not only illustrating Kropotkin's ideas
but building bridges through art between the past, present, and future?
Bonzo: I'm at least attempting that. I first read Mutual Aid when I was
much younger, just beginning to study anarchist writers. The world
Kropotkin discusses is irresistible—completely and utterly irresistible. It's
a world which has existed, which still exists, and which we have the power
to renew, even in places that have experienced extreme violent disruptions,
like Rojava.92

I wanted to capture this life-affirming, affinity-building spirit drawing
on illustration in the anarchist movement when Kropotkin wrote Mutual
Aid. I also looked to the Arts and Crafts and medieval styles of decoration
that Morris emulated when he created his books. I hope that I have contributed to the artistic traditions that inspired Kropotkin and made his vision
of an anarchist future as compelling for others as it is for me.

Artist's Note

The illustrations for this book were created over a period of more than two
years. They are directly inspired by the works of various members of the
Arts and Crafts movement and medieval woodblocks and engravings. Many
of the source materials referenced came from anarchist and socialist printing traditions in the late 1800s and early 1900s. A number of illustrations are
direct revivals of pieces from William Morris and Walter Crane. Their theories on aesthetic and everyday life were a major impetus for creating a work
that coupled illuminated texts with my enduring love for Kropotkin's text.
The full-page pieces represent the smallest potential representation
of mutual aid. There are hundreds upon thousands of examples that could
have been illustrated, and the influences displayed are in no way meant to
be singled out for special adulation, as collaboration and support are our
daily common experience. They are merely some of the most easily recognizable ones I have encountered as a punk and an anarchist.

As an artist, I have been lucky to have learned from a number of
vandals and printmakers. The majority of my influence within art has
come from friends and sometimes painting buddies, who I have watched
for years share techniques, process, support, and encouragement; who have
sacrificed their nights to watch friends' backs as they paint elaborate and
beautiful pieces on walls; who make bail fund pots when crewmates are
caught and endlessly distribute the supplies they rack; and who take longer
criminal sentences rather than reveal names to district attorneys.

The segregation of art from life, with art relegated to museums or
galleries and works of fine art priced beyond belief, is powerfully offset
by the work of vandals and movement printmakers. Works on the street
are accessible and immediate, an active and participatory part of everyday
life rather than being segregated from it. And movement printmakers craft
imagery to elevate and connect people in their shared commitments using
easily reproducible techniques meant to increase distribution and proliferation rather than limit it.

In that vein, the work in this book is meant for bootlegging, pirating,
flyering, putting on a zine cover, or whatever way people feel drawn to
engage. I hope to contribute, even minimally, to our shared project of a
collaborative and creative commons for all.

My work as an artist would be impossible without the people in my
life. Our conversations, commitments, and experiences all inform that work,
and it doesn't exist without them.

I want to extend my deepest and most loving appreciation to Mickey
and Danica. They have been my longest-enduring supports throughout the
making of this book. Additionally, I want to thank all of the artists who
have inspired me throughout the years and supported me as I developed.
Especially members of PTV and Few and Far, two of the most caring and
talented crews. Love to the radical printmakers and printers of Justseeds.
And extra thanks to GATS, Attica, Masher, and Amanda.

Thanks to antifascists, those who work collectively and act spontaneously to keep us safe. Portland would be a much different place without you.
Some people who I cannot get away without thanking for their friendship, jokes, and inspiring work are William G, Kevin Van Meter, Joey P,
Alana, Tighearnan, Maz, Aster, Duncan, Tiffany C., and Saiya.

I want to thank the staff at Raphael House of Portland for creating
unending space for me to pursue work that was fulfilling and for their constant support as we navigated spaces hell-bent on creating as many barriers
to safety and healing as possible for survivors.

I do not believe that the pieces I make would be possible without the
endless web of relationships I am immersed in and the constant exposure
to beautiful and radical ideas. Thanks to all the vandals, saboteurs, writers,
and all of us engaged in acts large and small of resistance.

And thank you, Chumbawamba—you're all I listened to while drawing.

Postscript

by GATS

When I was a young punk living in the middle of nowhere, I was rummaging through buildings I thought were abandoned. I found a box of old
pamphlets, one of which was about Food Not Bombs, a basic model for
starting a soup kitchen using salvaged produce.

A couple of friends and I tried to get it going to no avail because we
were trying to do a million projects at once. We moved to another town and
started working with a few more kids. It got a little more consistent but was
more like us handing out groceries.

We then moved to a larger town that had a pretty well-established Food
Not Bombs group. When I asked where everyone met to cook, I was told
the Catholic Workers House. I was immediately confused. "Like a church?"
I asked. I was a fresh atheist full of opinions at the time.

My friend said, "No, it's just like a big house with an industrial kitchen

where they help people."

I said, "But it's a Catholic spot?" My friend went on to explain to me
that the founder was Catholic but read Mutual Aid, and it inspired him to
break off from the church and build the Catholic Workers House.

This threw me off and I realized that I shouldn't judge people so fast
and that working together for a common good is what matters. I was familiar with the basic concept of mutual aid, but this was the experience that
made me pick up Peter Kropotkin's book.

Growing up through the 1980s and '90s, there was a very strong mentality of competition and dog-eat-dog, and I couldn't contextualize where it
came from. Reading Mutual Aid was like lifting a huge weight off my chest.
Maybe collaboration is the natural driving force of our existence and not
those brief moments of conflict that we put so much focus on.

After a couple of years of working with that Food Not Bombs and
overwhelming myself trying to organize way too many projects, I decided
to move to the Bay Area, where a lot of what I was interested in was already

going on. Not having to be the main organizer of these efforts, I started
focusing a lot more on art.

I had been making art my whole life but never considered myself an
artist until Rock Paper Scissors, a nonprofit gallery that organizes community programs, offered to host my first art show. No place I had lived before
ever had a gallery other than an unattainable museum. Artists working
together to maintain a physical space that none of them could have done
alone—this was the community that told me I was an artist.

When the Great Recession of 2008 hit, I lost my job and fell into being
an artist full time. The next five years were a network of artists shoplifting
food for each other, screen printing out of each other's basements, showering at each other's places, and building bed lofts in staircase hallways and
closets so that we could survive the winter. When one of us would get hurt,
we would all make a painting and throw a benefit art show because none
of us had health insurance.

This is why art almost always happens in movements. We have to build
off each other's collective knowledge and skills. Otherwise, we are all stuck
at figuring out stick figures and reinventing the wheel.

Illustration by GATS

Chapter i
1 Origin of Species, chap. iii.
2 Nineteenth Century, February 1888, 165.
3 Leaving aside the pre-Darwinian writers, like Toussenel, Fée, and many others, several works containing many striking instances of mutual aid—chiefly, however, illustrating animal intelligence were issued
previously to that date. I may mention those of Houzeau, Les facultés étales des animaux, 2 vols., Brussels,
1872; L. Büchner's Aus dem Geistesleben der Thiere, 2nd ed. in 1877; and Maximilian Perty Ueber das
Seelenleben der Thiere, Leipzig, 1876. Espinas published his most remarkable work, Les Sociétés animales,
in 1877, and in that work he pointed out the importance of animal societies, and their bearing upon
the preservation of species, and entered upon a most valuable discussion of the origin of societies. In
fact, Espinas book contains all that has been written since upon mutual aid and many good things
besides. If I nevertheless make a special mention of Kessler's address, it is because he raised mutual
aid to the height of a law much more important in evolution than the law of mutual struggle. The same
ideas were developed next year (in April 1881) by J. Lanessan in a lecture published in 1882 under this
title: La lutte pour l'existence et l'association pour la lutte. G. Romanes's capital work, Animal Intelligence,
was issued in 1882, and followed next year by Mental Evolution in Animals. About the same time (1883),

Büchner published another work, Liebe und Liebes-Leben in der Thierwelt, a second edition of which
was issued in 1885. The idea, as seen, was in the air.

4 Memoirs (Trudy) of the St. Petersburg Society of Naturalists, vol. xi. 1880.
5 See Appendix I.
6 George J. Romanes, Animal Intelligence, 1st ed., 233.
7 Pierre Huber's Les fourmis indigènes, Génève, 1861; Forel's Recherches sur les fourmis de la Suisse, Zurich,
1874, and J.T. Moggridge Harvesting Ants and Trapdoor Spiders, London, 1873 and 1874, ought to be in
the hands of every boy and girl. See also Blanchard's MéMétamorphoses des Insectes, Paris, 1868; J.H. Fabre
Souvenirs entomologiques, Paris, 1886; Ebrard's Etudes des moeurs des fourmis, Génève, 1864; Sir John
Lubbock's Ants, Bees, and Wasps, and so on.

8 Forel's Recherches, 244, 275, 278. Huber's description of the process is admirable. It also contains a hint

as to the possible origin of the instinct (popular edition, 158, 160). See Appendix II.

9 The agriculture of the ants is so wonderful that for a long time it has been doubted. The fact is now so
well proved by Mr. Moggridge, Dr. Lincecum, Mr. MacCook, Col. Sykes, and Dr. Jerdon, that no doubt is
possible. See an excellent summary of evidence in Mr. Romanes's work. See also Die Pilzgaerten einiger
Süd-Amerikanischen Ameisen, by Alf. Moeller, in Schimper's Botan. Mitth. aus den Tropen, vi. 1893.
10 This second principle was not recognized at once. Former observers often spoke of kings, queens,
managers, and so on; but since Huber and Forel have published their minute observations, no doubt
is possible as to the free scope left for every individual's initiative in whatever the ants do, including
their wars.

11 H.W. Bates, The Naturalist on the River Amazons, ii. 59 seq.
12 N. Syevertsoff, Periodical Phenomena in the Life of Mammalia, Birds, and Reptiles of Voronèje, Moscow,

1855 (in Russian).

13 A. Brehm, Life of Animals, iii. 477; all quotations after the French edition.
14 Bates, 1.

During their migrations, birds of prey often associate. One flock, which H. Seebohm saw crossing the
Pyrenees, represented a curious assemblage of "eight kites, one crane, and a peregrine falcon" (The
Birds of Siberia, 1901, 417).
16 Birds in the Northern Shires, 207.

2, 11.

20 Brehm, iv. 567.
21 As to the house-sparrows, a New Zealand observer, Mr. T.W. Kirk, described as follows the attack of
these "impudent" birds upon an "unfortunate" hawk.—"He heard one day a most unusual noise, as
though all the small birds of the country had joined in one grand quarrel. Looking up, he saw a large
hawk (C. gouldi—a carrion feeder) being buffeted by a flock of sparrows. They kept dashing at him in
scores, and from all points at once. The unfortunate hawk was quite powerless. At last, approaching
some scrub, the hawk dashed into it and remained there, while the sparrows congregated in groups
round the bush, keeping up a constant chattering and noise" (Paper read before the New Zealand
Institute; Nature, October 10, 1891).

22 Brehm, iv. 671 seq.

Chapter ii
1 Syevettsoff 's Periodical Phenomena, 251.
2 Seyfferlitz, quoted by Brehm, iv. 760.
3 The Arctic Voyages of A.E. Nordenskjöld, London, 1879, 135. See also the powerful description of the St.

Kilda islands by Mr. Dixon (quoted by Seebohm), and nearly all books of Arctic travel.

4 See Appendix III.
5 Elliott Couës, in Bulletin U.S. Geol. Survey of Territories, iv. no. 7, 556, 579, etc. Among the gulls (Larus
argentatus), Polyakoff saw on a marsh in Northern Russia, that the nesting grounds of a very great
number of these birds were always patrolled by one male, which warned the colony of the approach
of danger. All birds rose in such case and attacked the enemy with great vigour. The females, which
had five or six nests together on each knoll of the marsh, kept a certain order in leaving their nests in
search of food. The fledglings, which otherwise are extremely unprotected and easily become the prey
of the rapacious birds, were never left alone ("Family Habits among the Aquatic Birds," in Proceedings
of the Zool. Section of St. Petersburg Society of Naturalists, December 17, 1874).

6 Brehm Father, quoted by A. Brehm, iv. 34 seq. See also White's Natural History of Selborne, letter XI.
7 Dr. Couës, Birds of Dakota and Montana, in Bulletin U.S. Survey of Territories, iv. no. 7.

It has often been intimated that larger birds may occasionally transport some of the smaller birds when
they cross together the Mediterranean, but the fact still remains doubtful. On the other side, it is certain
that some smaller birds join the bigger ones for migration. The fact has been noticed several times, and
it was recently confirmed by L. Buxbaum at Raunheim. He saw several parties of cranes which had larks
flying in the midst and on both sides of their migratory columns (Der zoologische Garten, 1886, 133).

9 H. Seebohm and Ch. Dixon both mention this habit.
10 The fact is well known to every field-naturalist, and with reference to England several examples may
be found in Charles Dixon's Among the Birds in Northern Shires. The chaffinches arrive during winter
in vast flocks; and about the same time, i.e. in November, come flocks of bramblings; redwings also
frequent the same places "in similar large companies," and so on (165, 166).

11 S.W. Baker, Wild Beasts, etc., vol. i, 316.
12 Tschudi, Thierleben der Alpenwelt, 404.
13 Houzeau's Études, ii, 463.
14 For their hunting associations see Sir E. Tennant's Natural History of Ceylon, quoted in Romanes's

Animal Intelligence, 432.

15 See Emil Hüter's letter in L. Büchner's Liebe.
16 See Appendix IV.
17 With regard to the viscacha it is very interesting to note that these highly sociable little animals not
only live peaceably together in each village, but that whole villages visit each other at nights. Sociability
is thus extended to the whole species—not only to a given society, or to a nation, as we saw it with the
ants. When the farmer destroys a viscacha-burrow, and buries the inhabitants under a heap of earth,
other viscachas—we are told by Hudson—"come from a distance to dig out those that are buried alive"
(l.c., 311). This is a widely known fact in La Plata, verified by the author.

18 Handbuch für Jäger und Jagdberechtigte, quoted by Brehm, ii. 223.

19 Buffon's Histoire Naturelle.
20 In connection with the horses it is worthy of notice that the quagga zebra, which never comes together
with the dauw zebra, nevertheless lives on excellent terms, not only with ostriches, which are very
good sentries, but also with gazelles, several species of antelopes, and gnus. We thus have a case of
mutual dislike between the quagga and the dauw which cannot be explained by competition for food.
The fact that the quagga lives together with ruminants feeding on the same grass as itself excludes
that hypothesis, and we must look for some incompatibility of character, as in the case of the hare and
the rabbit. Cf., among others, Clive Phillips-Wolley's Big Game Shooting (Badminton Library), which
contains excellent illustrations of various species living together in East Africa.

21 Our Tungus hunter, who was going to marry, and therefore was prompted by the desire of getting as
many furs as he possibly could, was beating the hill-sides all day long on horseback in search of deer.
His efforts were not rewarded by even so much as one fallow deer killed every day; and he was an
excellent hunter.

22 According to Samuel W. Baker, elephants combine in larger groups than the "compound family." "I
have frequently observed," he wrote, "in the portion of Ceylon known as the Park Country, the tracks
of elephants in great numbers which have evidently been considerable herds that have joined together
in a general retreat from a ground which they considered insecure" (Wild Beasts and Their Ways, vol. i,
102).

23 Pigs, attacked by wolves, do the same (Hudson, l.c.).
24 Romanes's Animal Intelligence, 472.
25 Brehm, i. 82; Darwin's Descent of Man, chap. iii. The Kozloff expedition of 1899–1901 have also had to

sustain in Northern Thibet a similar fight.

26 The more strange was it to read in the previously mentioned article by Huxley the following paraphrase
of a well-known sentence of Rousseau: "The first men who substituted mutual peace for that of mutual
war—whatever the motive which impelled them to take that step—created society" (Nineteenth Century,
February 1888, 165). Society has not been created by man; it is anterior to man.

27 Such monographs as the chapter on "Music and Dancing in Nature" which we have in Hudson's
Naturalist on the La Plata, and Carl Gross's Play of Animals, have already thrown a considerable light
upon an instinct which is absolutely universal in Nature.

28 Not only numerous species of birds possess the habit of assembling together—in many cases always at
the same spot—to indulge in antics and dancing performances, but W.H. Hudson's experience is that
nearly all mammals and birds ("probably there are really no exceptions") indulge frequently in more
or less regular or set performances with or without sound, or composed of sound exclusively (264).

29 For the choruses of monkeys, see Brehm.
30 Haygarth, Bush Life in Australia, 58.
31 To quote but a few instances, a wounded badger was carried away by another badger suddenly appearing on the scene; rats have been seen feeding a blind couple (Seelenleben der Thiere, 64 seq.). Brehm
himself saw two crows feeding in a hollow tree a third crow which was wounded; its wound was several
weeks old (Hausfreund, 1874, 715; Büchner's Liebe, 203). Mr. Blyth saw Indian crows feeding two or three
blind comrades; and so on.

32 Man and Beast, 344.
33 L.H. Morgan, The American Beaver, 1868, 272; Descent of Man, chap. iv.

34 One species of swallow is said to have caused the decrease of another swallow species in North America;
the recent increase of the missel-thrush in Scotland has caused the decrease of the song-thrush; the
brown rat has taken the place of the black rat in Europe; in Russia the small cockroach has everywhere
driven before it its greater congener; and in Australia the imported hive-bee is rapidly exterminating
the small stingless bee. Two other cases, but relative to domesticated animals, are mentioned in the
preceding paragraph. While recalling these same facts, A.R. Wallace remarks in a footnote relative to
the Scottish thrushes: "Prof. A. Newton, however, informs me that these species do not interfere in
the way here stated" (Darwinism, 34). As to the brown rat, it is known that, owing to its amphibian
habits, it usually stays in the lower parts of human dwellings (low cellars, sewers, etc.), as also on the
banks of canals and rivers; it also undertakes distant migrations in numberless bands. The black rat,
on the contrary, prefers staying in our dwellings themselves, under the floor, as well as in our stables
and barns. It thus is much more exposed to be exterminated by man; and we cannot maintain, with
any approach to certainty, that the black rat is being either exterminated or starved out by the brown
rat and not by man.

35 "But it may be urged that when several closely allied species inhabit the same territory, we surely ought
to find at the present time many transitional forms. . . . By my theory these allied species are descended
from a common parent; and during the process of modification, each has become adapted to the conditions of life of its own region, and has supplanted and exterminated its original parent-form and all
the transitional varieties between its past and present states" (Origin of Species, 6th ed., 134); also 137,
296 (all paragraph "On Extinction").

36 According to Madame Marie Pavloff, who has made a special study of this subject, they migrated from
Asia to Africa, stayed there some time, and returned next to Asia. Whether this double migration be
confirmed or not, the fact of a former extension of the ancestor of our horse over Asia, Africa, and
America is settled beyond doubt.

37 The Naturalist on the River Amazons, ii. 85, 95.
38 Dr. B. Altum, Waldbeschädigungen durch Thiere und Gegenmittel (Berlin, 1889), 207 seq.
39 Dr. B. Altum, ut supra, 13 and 187.
40 A. Becker in the Bulletin de la Société des Naturalistes de Moscou, 1889, 625.
41 See Appendix V.
42 Russkaya Mysl, September 1888: "The Theory of Beneficency of Struggle for Life, being a Preface to

various Treatises on Botanics, Zoology, and Human Life," by an Old Transformist.

43 "One of the most frequent modes in which Natural Selection acts is, by adapting some individuals of
a species to a somewhat different mode of life, whereby they are able to seize unappropriated places
in Nature" (Origin of Species, 145) in other words, to avoid competition.

44 See Appendix VI.
Chapter iii
1 Nineteenth Century, February 1888, 165.
2 The Descent of Man, end of chap. ii, 63 and 64 of the 2nd ed.
3 Anthropologists who fully endorse the above views as regards man nevertheless intimate, sometimes,
that the apes live in polygamous families, under the leadership of "a strong and jealous male." I do not
know how far that assertion is based upon conclusive observation. But the passage from Brehm's Life of
Animals, which is sometimes referred to, can hardly be taken as very conclusive. It occurs in his general

description of monkeys; but his more detailed descriptions of separate species either contradict it or
do not confirm it. Even as regards the cercopithèques, Brehm is affirmative in saying that they "nearly
always live in bands, and very seldom in families" (French edition, 59). As to other species, the very
numbers of their bands, always containing many males, render the "polygamous family" more than
doubtful further observation is evidently wanted.

4 Lubbock, Prehistoric Times, 5th ed., 1890.
5 That extension of the ice-cap is admitted by most of the geologists who have specially studied the glacial
age. The Russian Geological Survey already has taken this view as regards Russia, and most German
specialists maintain it as regards Germany. The glaciation of most of the central plateau of France will
not fail to be recognized by the French geologists, when they pay more attention to the glacial deposits
altogether.

6 Prehistoric Times, 232 and 242.
7 Bachofen, Das Mutterrecht, Stuttgart, 1861; Lewis H. Morgan, Ancient Society, or Researches in the Lines
of Human Progress from Savagery through Barbarism to Civilization, New York, 1877; J.F. MacLennan,
Studies in Ancient History, 1st series, new edition, 1886; 2nd series, 1896; L. Fison and A.W. Howitt,
Kamilaroi and Kurnai, Melbourne. These four writers—as has been very truly remarked by Giraud
Teulon—starting from different facts and different general ideas, and following different methods, have
come to the same conclusion. To Bachofen we owe the notion of the maternal family and the maternal
succession; to Morgan—the system of kinship, Malayan and Turanian, and a highly gifted sketch of the
main phases of human evolution; to MacLennan—the law of exogeny; and to Fison and Howitt—the
cuadro, or scheme, of the conjugal societies in Australia. All four end in establishing the same fact of
the tribal origin of the family. When Bachofen first drew attention to the maternal family, in his epochmaking work, and Morgan described the clan-organization—both concurring to the almost general
extension of these forms and maintaining that the marriage laws lie at the very basis of the consecutive
steps of human evolution, they were accused of exaggeration. However, the most careful researches
prosecuted since, by a phalanx of students of ancient law, have proved that all races of mankind bear
traces of having passed through similar stages of development of marriage laws, such as we now see in
force among certain savages. See the works of Post, Dargun, Kovalevsky, Lubbock, and their numerous
followers: Lippert, Mucke, etc.

8 See Appendix VII.
9 For the Semites and the Aryans, see especially Prof. Maxim Kovalevsky's Primitive Law (in Russian),
Moscow, 1886 and 1887. Also his Lectures delivered at Stockholm (Tableau des origines et de l' évolution
de la famille et de la propriété, Stockholm, 1890), which represents an admirable review of the whole
question. Cf. also A. Post, Die Geschlechtsgenossenschaft der Urzeit, Oldenburg 1875.

10 It would be impossible to enter here into a discussion of the origin of the marriage restrictions. Let me
only remark that a division into groups, similar to Morgan's Hawaian, exists among birds; the young
broods live together separately from their parents. A like division might probably be traced among some
mammals as well. As to the prohibition of relations between brothers and sisters, it is more likely to
have arisen, not from speculations about the bad effects of consanguinity, which speculations really
do not seem probable, but to avoid the too-easy precocity of like marriages. Under close cohabitation
it must have become of imperious necessity. I must also remark that in discussing the origin of new
customs altogether, we must keep in mind that the savages, like us, have their "thinkers" and savants—
wizards, doctors, prophets, etc.—whose knowledge and ideas are in advance upon those of the masses.

United as they are in their secret unions (another almost universal feature) they are certainly capable of
exercising a powerful influence, and of enforcing customs the utility of which may not yet be recognized
by the majority of the tribe.

11 Col. Collins, in Philips's Researches in South Africa, London, 1828. Quoted by Waitz, Anthropologie der

Naturvolker, vol. ii. 334.

12 Lichtenstein's Reisen im südlichen Afrika, ii. 92, 97. Berlin, 1811.
13 Waitz, vol. ii. 335 seq. See also Fritsch's Die Eingeboren Afrika's, Breslau, 1872, 386 seq.; and Drei Jahre

in Süd Afrika. Also W. Bleck, A Brief Account of Bushmen Folklore, Capetown, 1875.

1731, vol. i. 59, 71, 333, 336, etc.

16 Quoted in Waitz's Anthropologie, vol. ii. 335 seq.
17 The natives living in the north of Sidney, and speaking the Kamilaroi language, are best known under

20 Bulletin de la Société d'Anthropologie, 1888, vol. xi, 652. I abridge the answers.
21 Bulletin de la Société d'Anthropologie, 1888, vol. xi, 386.
22 The same is the practice with the Papuas of Kaimani Bay, who have a high reputation of honesty. "It
never happens that the Papua be untrue to his promise," Finsch says in Neuguinea und seine Bewohner,
Bremen, 1865, 829.

23 Izvestia of the Russian Geographical Society, 1880, 161 seq. Few books of travel give a better insight into

the petty details of the daily life of savages than these scraps from Maklay's notebooks.

24 L.F. Martial, in Mission Scientifique au Cap Horn, Paris, 1883, vol. i. 183–201.
25 Captain Holm's Expedition to East Greenland.
26 In Australia whole clans have been seen exchanging all their wives, in order to conjure a calamity (Post,
Studien zur Entwicklungsgeschichte des Familienrechts, 1890, 342). More brotherhood is their specific
against calamities.

27 Dr. H. Rink, The Eskimo Tribes, 26 (Meddelelser om Grönland, vol. xi. 1887).
28 Dr. Rink, loc. cit., 24. Europeans, grown in the respect of Roman law, are seldom capable of understanding that force of tribal authority. "In fact," Dr. Rink writes, "it is not the exception, but the rule,
that white men who have stayed for ten or twenty years among the Eskimo, return without any real
addition to their knowledge of the traditional ideas upon which their social state is based. The white
man, whether a missionary or a trader, is firm in his dogmatic opinion that the most vulgar European
is better than the most distinguished native." The Eskimo Tribes, 31.

29 Dall, Alaska and its Resources, Cambridge, U.S., 1870.
30 Dall saw it in Alaska, Jacobsen at Ignitok in the vicinity of the Bering Strait. Gilbert Sproat mentions it
among the Vancouver Indians; and Dr. Rink, who describes the periodical exhibitions just mentioned,

adds: "The principal use of the accumulation of personal wealth is for periodically distributing it." He
also mentions (loc. cit., 31) "the destruction of property for the same purpose,' (of maintaining equality).

31 See Appendix VIII.
32 Veniaminoff, Memoirs relative to the District of Unalashka (Russian), 3 vols. St. Petersburg, 1840. Extracts,
in English, from the above are given in Dall's Alaska. A like description of the Australians' morality is
given in Nature, xlii, 639.

33 It is most remarkable that several writers (Middendorff, Schrenk, O. Finsch) described the Ostyaks
and Samoyedes in almost the same words. Even when drunken, their quarrels are insignificant. "For
a hundred years one single murder has been committed in the tundra"; "their children never fight";
"anything may be left for years in the tundra, even food and gin, and nobody will touch it"; and so on.
Gilbert Sproat "never witnessed a fight between two sober natives" of the Aht Indians of Vancouver
Island. "Quarrelling is also rare among their children." (Rink, loc. cit.) And so on.

34 Gill, quoted in Gerland and Waitz's Anthropologie, vol. v. 641. See also 636–640, where many facts of

parental and filial love are quoted.

35 Primitive Folk, London, 1891.
36 Gerland, loc. cit. v. 636.
37 Erskine, quoted in Gerland and Waitz's Anthropologie, vol. v. 640.
38 W.T. Pritchard, Polynesian Reminiscences, London, 1866, 363.
39 It is remarkable, however, that in case of a sentence of death, nobody will take upon himself to be the
executioner. Every one throws his stone, or gives his blow with the hatchet, carefully avoiding to give
a mortal blow. At a later epoch, the priest will stab the victim with a sacred knife. Still later, it will be
the king, until civilization invents the hired hangman. See Bastian's deep remarks upon this subject
in Der Mensch in der Geschichte, iii. Die Blutrache, 1–36. A remainder of this tribal habit, I am told by
Professor E. Nys, has survived in military executions till our own times. In the middle portion of the
nineteenth century it was the habit to load the rifles of the twelve soldiers called out for shooting the
condemned victim, with eleven ball-cartridges and one blank cartridge. As the soldiers never knew who
of them had the latter, each one could console his disturbed conscience by thinking that he was not one
of the murderers.

40 In Africa, and elsewhere too, it is a widely spread habit, that if a theft has been committed, the next clan
has to restore the equivalent of the stolen thing, and then look itself for the thief. A.H. Post, Afrikanische
Jurisprudenz, Leipzig, 1887, vol. i, 77.

41 See Prof. M. Kovalevsky's Modern Customs and Ancient Law (Russian), Moscow, 1886, vol. ii., which

contains many important considerations upon this subject.

42 See Carl Bock, The Head Hunters of Borneo, London, 1881. I am told, however, by Sir Hugh Law, who
was for a long time Governor of Borneo, that the "head-hunting" described in this book is grossly exaggerated. Altogether, my informant speaks of the Dayaks in exactly the same sympathetic terms as Ida
Pfeiffer. Let me add that Mary Kingsley speaks in her book on West Africa in the same sympathetic
terms of the Fans, who had been represented formerly as the most "terrible cannibals."

43 Ida Pfeiffer, Meine zweite Weltrieze, Wien, 1856, vol. i. 116 seq. See also Müller and Temminch's Dutch

Possessions in Archipelagic India, quoted by Elisée Reclus, in Géographie Universelle, xiii.

44 Descent of Man, 2nd ed., 63, 64.
45 See Bastian's Mensch in der Geschichte, iii, 7. Also Grey, loc. cit. ii, 238.
46 Miklukho-Maclay, loc. cit. Same habit with the Hottentots.

Chapter iv
1 Numberless traces of post-pliocene lakes, now disappeared, are found over Central, West, and North
Asia. Shells of the same species as those now found in the Caspian Sea are scattered over the surface
of the soil as far East as half-way to Lake Aral, and are found in recent deposits as far north as Kazan.
Traces of Caspian Gulfs, formerly taken for old beds of the Amu, intersect the Turcoman territory.
Deduction must surely be made for temporary, periodical oscillations. But with all that, desiccation is
evident, and it progresses at a formerly unexpected speed. Even in the relatively wet parts of South-
West Siberia, the succession of reliable surveys, recently published by Yadrintseff, shows that villages
have grown up on what was, eighty years ago, the bottom of one of the lakes of the Tchany group; while
the other lakes of the same group, which covered hundreds of square miles some fifty years ago, are now
mere ponds. In short, the desiccation of North-West Asia goes on at a rate which must be measured by
centuries, instead of by the geological units of time of which we formerly used to speak.

2 Whole civilizations had thus disappeared, as is proved now by the remarkable discoveries in Mongolia

on the Orkhon and in the Lukchun depression (by Dmitri Clements).
If I follow the opinions of (to name modern specialists only) Nasse, Kovalevsky, and Vinogradov, and
not those of Mr. Seebohm (Mr. Denman Ross can only be named for the sake of completeness), it is
not only because of the deep knowledge and concordance of views of these three writers, but also on
account of their perfect knowledge of the village community altogether—a knowledge the want of
which is much felt in the otherwise remarkable work of Mr. Seebohm. The same remark applies, in a
still higher degree, to the most elegant writings of Fustel de Coulanges, whose opinions and passionate
interpretations of old texts are confined to himself.

4 The literature of the village community is so vast that but a few works can be named. Those of Sir
Henry Maine, Mr. Seebohm, and Walter's Das alte Wallis (Bonn, 1859), are well-known popular sources
of information about Scotland, Ireland, and Wales. For France, Viollet, Précis de l'histoire du droit français.
Droit privé, 1886, and several of his monographs in Bibl. de l'Ecole des Chartes; Babeau, Le Village sous
l'ancien régime (the mir in the eighteenth century), 3rd ed., 1887; Bonnemère, Doniol, etc. For Italy and
Scandinavia, the chief works are named in Laveleye's Primitive Property, German version by K. Bücher.
For the Finns, Rein's FöreläFöreläsningar, i. 16; Koskinen, Finnische Geschichte, 1874, and various monographs.
For the Lives and Coures, Prof. Lutchitzky in Severnyi Vestnil, 1891. For the Teutons, besides the
well-known works of Maurer, Sohm (Altdeutsche Reichs- und Gerichts- Verfassung), also Dahn (Urzeit,
Vökerwanderung, Langobardische Studien), Janssen, Wilh. Arnold, etc. For India, besides H. Maine and
the works he names, Sir John Phear's Aryan Village. For Russia and South Slavonians, see Kavelin,
Posnikoff, Sokolovsky, Kovalevsky, Efimenko, Ivanisheff, Klaus, etc. (copious bibliographical index up
to 1880 in the Sbornik svedeniy ob obschinye of the Russ. Geog. Soc.). For general conclusions, besides
Laveleye's Propriété, Morgan's Ancient Society, Lippert's Kulturgeschichte, Post, Dargun, etc., also the
lectures of M. Kovalevsky (Tableau des origines et de l'évolution de la famille et de la propriété, Stockholm,
1890). Many special monographs ought to be mentioned; their titles may be found in the excellent lists
given by Viollet in Droit privé and Droit public. For other races, see subsequent notes.

5 Several authorities are inclined to consider the joint household as an intermediate stage between the
clan and the village community; and there is no doubt that in very many cases village communities have
grown up out of undivided families. Nevertheless, I consider the joint household as a fact of a different
order. We find it within the gentes; on the other hand, we cannot affirm that joint families have existed

at any period without belonging either to a gens or to a village community, or to a Gau. I conceive the
early village communities as slowly originating directly from the gentes, and consisting, according to
racial and local circumstances, either of several joint families, or of both joint and simple families, or
(especially in the case of new settlements) of simple families only. If this view be correct, we should
not have the right of establishing the series: gens, compound family, village community—the second
member of the series having not the same ethnological value as the two others. See Appendix IX.

6 Stobbe, Beiträg zur Geschichte des deutschen Rechtes, 62.
7 The few traces of private property in land which are met with in the early barbarian period are found
with such stems (the Batavians, the Franks in Gaul) as have been for a time under the influence of
Imperial Rome. See Inama-Sternegg's Die Ausbildung der grossen Grundherrschaften in Deutschland, Bd.
i. 1878. Also, Besseler, Neubruch nach dem Älteren deutschen Recht, 11–12, quoted by Kovalevsky, Modern
Custom and Ancient Law, Moscow, 1886, i. 134.

8 Maurer's Markgenossenschaft; Lamprecht's "Wirthschaft und Recht der Franken zur Zeit der Volksrechte,"

in Histor. Taschenbuch, 1883; Seebohm's The English Village Community, chaps. VI, VII, and IX.

9 Letourneau, in Bulletin de la Soc. D'Anthropologie, 1888, vol. xi, 476.
10 Walter, Das alte Wallis, 323; Dm. Bakradze and N. Khoudadoff in Russian Zapiski of the Caucasian Geogr.

Society, xiv. Part I.

11 Bancroft's Native Races; Waitz, Anthropologie, vol. iii. 423; Montrozier, in Bull. Soc. D'Anthropologie, 1870;

Post's Studien, etc.

12 A number of works, by Ory, Luro, Laudes, and Sylvestre, on the village community in Annam, proving
that it has had there the same forms as in Germany or Russia, are mentioned in a review of these works
by Jobbéé-Duval, in Nouvelle Revue historique de droit français et étranger, October and December 1896. A
good study of the village community of Peru, before the establishment of the power of the Incas, has
been brought out by Heinrich Cunow (Die Soziale Verfassung des Inka-Reichs, Stuttgart, 1896. The communal possession of land and communal culture are described in that work.

13 Kovalevsky, Modern Custom and Ancient Law, i. 115.
14 Palfrey, History of New England, ii. 13; quoted in Maine's Village Communities, New York, 1876, 201.
15 Königswarter, Etudes sur le dédéveloppement des sociétés humaines, Paris, 1850.
16 This is, at least, the law of the Kalmucks, whose customary law bears the closest resemblance to the

laws of the Teutons, the old Slavonians, etc.

17 The habit is in force still with many African and other tribes.
18 Village Communities, 65–68 and 199.
19 Maurer (Gesch. Der Markverfassung, secs. 29, 97) is quite decisive upon this subject. He maintains that
"All members of the community . . . the laic and clerical lords as well, often also the partial co-possessors
(Markberechtigte), and even strangers to the Mark, were submitted to its jurisdiction" (312). This conception remained locally in force up to the fifteenth century.

20 Königswarter, loc. cit., 50; J. Thrupp, Historical Law Tracts, London, 1843, 106.
21 Königswarter has shown that the fred originated from an offering which had to be made to appease the
ancestors. Later on, it was paid to the community, for the breach of peace; and still later to the judge,
or king, or lord, when they had appropriated to themselves the rights of the community.

22 Post's Bausteine and Afrikanische Jurisprudenz, Oldenburg, 1887, vol. i. 64 seq.; Kovalevsky, loc. cit. ii.

164–189.

23 O. Miller and M. Kovalevsky, "In the Mountaineer Communities of Kabardia," in Vestnik Evropy, April
1884. With the Shakhsevens of the Mugan Steppe, blood feuds always end by marriage between the
two hostile sides (Markoff, in appendix to the Zapiski of the Caucasian Geogr. Soc. xiv. 1, 21).

24 Post, in Afrik. Jurisprudenz, gives a series of facts illustrating the conceptions of equity inrooted among
the African barbarians. The same may be said of all serious examinations into barbarian common law.
25 See the excellent chapter "Le droit de La Vieille Irlande" (also "Le Haut Nord") in Études de droit international et de droit politique, by Prof. E. Nys, Bruxelles, 1896.

26 Introduction, xxxv.
27 Das alte Wallis, 343–350.

29 Henry Maine, International Law, London, 1888, 11–13. E. Nys, Les origines du droit international, Bruxelles,

1894.

30 A Russian historian, the Kazan Professor Schapoff, who was exiled in 1862 to Siberia, has given a good
